<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01X2Hmx8VinJBShaqxcwDd5a`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM agent routing (deciding when to rely on internal reasoning versus external tool use) is typically addressed by training routers directly, without first understanding what mechanistic signal drives the routing preference. This absence of diagnostic methods risks building routers atop confounded or trivial signal. We propose an episode-level counterfactual framework that compares pure-strategy outcomes (internal-only vs.\ deterministic-only) to decompose routing signal into its mechanistic components before any router is constructed. Applying this framework to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes), we find that 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry---a finding replicated across three architecturally distinct 7--9B model families (spread 2.2\,pp), revealing that in catastrophic-failure environments, signal diversity can collapse to a single dominant mechanism. Cross-environment probes on MATH and ALFWorld diagnose qualitatively different routing mechanisms (no extractable signal and information asymmetry, respectively), providing preliminary evidence of applicability beyond death-dominated settings. The framework distinguishes \emph{methodological generality} (applicable to any environment with paired episode outcomes) from \emph{empirical generality} (full quantitative validation on ScienceWorld only). A ${\sim}$50-episode diagnostic pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$); environments with smaller effects may require larger samples.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01K3eYvMaL6mRctDAtcpESUS`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset, not to all ScienceWorld tasks. An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +32.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.
5	
6	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
7	\label{sec:death-decomp}
8	
9	\begin{figure}[t]
10	\centering
11	\includegraphics[width=0.38\textwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
12	\caption{Internal consistency check: per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\ performance gap ($\Delta_{\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$ (Spearman's $\rho = 0.986$; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}), $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types. Since $\Delta_{\text{full}} \approx \delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ with $P$ constant, $r \to 1$ as DAF $\to 1$; the near-perfect correlation is a structural consequence of death dominance, not an independent finding (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).}
13	\label{fig:decomposition}
14	\end{figure}
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\% CI: $[81.1\%, 99.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\%$ at $P{=}10$; $82.6\%$ at $P{=}50$; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \to 0$, DAF $\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\geq}3$; \S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.
19	
20	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
21	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$). Per-task death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}); since DAF$\to 1$ implies $r \to 1$ by construction, the near-perfect correlation is a consistency check, not an independent finding (Appendix~\ref{app:correlation}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
22	
23	\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}
24	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\% death-asymmetry labels, while \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores) produces 0\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\% and yields DAF $\geq$100\% ($\Delta_\text{nd} \leq 0$; death avoidance fully accounts for the routing gap among remaining task types; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}).
25	
26	\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}
27	Non-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.1\%, 99.0\%]$ for DAF; cluster-robust CI $[73.3\%, 100.0\%]$ ($K{=}11$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}), with Cohen's $d = 3.02$ (interpret cautiously given $K{=}11$ task types). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\%--78.9\%; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}); an independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\%$, within 0.7~pp (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p = 0.002$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}) provide supplementary evidence, though $K < 20$ limits cluster-level inference~\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}.
28	
29	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation (7--9B Tier)}
30	\label{sec:cross-model}
31	
32	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} cross-model replication across all 11 task types ($N{=}220$) confirms that death dominance replicates across model families: internal death rate $41.4\%$ vs.\ deterministic $11.4\%$ (death-rate differential $+30.0$~pp), DAF $= 88.2\%$ (95\% CI: $[80.2\%, 95.1\%]$; $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +33.17$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +3.9$), with direction consistency 10/10 on death-prone tasks, 83 deterministic-preferred, 3 internal-preferred (1.4\%, all death-asymmetry reversals), and 134 no-preference labels.
33	Both models show ${\sim}42\%$ internal vulnerability; the differential magnitude varies ($+26.5$ vs.\ $+30.0$~pp) due to model-dependent deterministic safety (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
34	Per-task details, including cross-model precision matching and routing transfer analysis, appear in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}.
35	A third model family, Gemma-2-9B-it~\citep{gemma2-2024} (sliding window attention, vs.\ full attention in Qwen/Llama), independently confirms the pattern: DAF${=}89.7\%$ (95\% CI $[79.5\%, 98.4\%]$; $N{=}220$), with all 10 death-prone task types showing positive $\Delta$DR (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). Three architecturally distinct models thus converge on DAF $88$--$90\%$ (spread 2.2~pp); $10{\times}$ scaling to 72B (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) confirms persistence across capability levels.
```

```text
14	\label{sec:problem-setup}
15	
16	Consider an interactive environment with observation space $\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\pi: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\mathcal{M} = \{{\tt int}, {\tt det}\}$: \textbf{internal} (${\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\citep{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \textbf{deterministic} (${\tt det}$), a privileged-information strategy that eliminates observation uncertainty---instantiated as oracle simulator (ScienceWorld), code interpreter (MATH), or admissible-action enumeration (ALFWorld); these qualitative differences affect what routing signals the framework can detect. A \emph{routing policy} $\rho: \mathcal{O}^* \rightarrow \mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\rho$.
17	
18	\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.
5	Self-supervised methods learn tool invocation from loss signals \citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, token-level methods embed tools in the vocabulary \citep{toolkengpt-2023}, task-level decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules \citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based methods learn routing from reward \citep{retool-2025}.
6	All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
7	Concurrent work examines \emph{when} to invoke tools: tool-call decisions correlate with uncertainty~\citep{tool-overuse-illusion-2026}, are encoded in hidden states~\citep{when2tool-2026}, impose a ``tool-use tax''~\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, or follow necessity-utility-affordability logic~\citep{to-call-or-not-2026}. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
8	
9	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
10	Adaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \citep{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \citep{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \citep{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.
11	RouteLLM \citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.
12	\citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing policies under sparse terminal rewards, the closest setting to ours; they optimize \emph{which} model to invoke, whereas we diagnose \emph{what} drives the routing decision.
13	Mixture-of-Experts architectures~\citep{switch-transformer-2022,gshard-2021} learn token-level routing across expert sub-networks; we route between computation \emph{modes} rather than expert modules. Counterfactual data augmentation~\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs to flip labels; our framework executes full counterfactual \emph{episodes} to decompose routing signal at the environment level.
14	
15	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}
16	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions \citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}; recent extensions target LLM agents~\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \emph{before} training.
17	
18	\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}
19	ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \citep{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary environment; AgentBench \citep{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. ReAct \citep{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \citep{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \citep{lats-2024} adds tree search. SALT~\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.
20	
21	\paragraph{Safe RL.}
22	Death avoidance connects to constrained MDPs \citep{altman1999constrained}, risk-sensitive RL \citep{mihatsch2002risk}, and safe RL \citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}; however, classical methods assume known dynamics or differentiable constraints, neither available for LLM agents. \citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions as a failure category; SafeRoute \citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety; Agent-SafetyBench \citep{agent-safetybench-2024} evaluates agent safety failures systematically.
23	
24	\paragraph{Causal Grounding and Agent Safety.}
25	Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified instance of the potential outcomes framework~\citep{rubin1974estimating}: two treatments assigned under controlled conditions, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\citep{pearl2009causality}. \citet{bonagiri2025check} show that selective quitting yields safety gains; our framework explains \emph{why}---death avoidance dominates routing signal in catastrophic-failure environments.
```

_source line 550 · 2026-05-22T05:21:07.939Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MGcDsW6uGw2Ecv744ccYXC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	In ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation, internal reasoning is error-prone, as the model may hallucinate state changes (e.g., that water has boiled when the stove was never activated), while deterministic external computation eliminates such errors via exact state transitions. The practical question: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision: Toolformer \citep{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls by perplexity reduction; ART \citep{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \citep{chameleon-2023} selects heterogeneous modules per subtask; adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \citep{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026}, model tiers \citep{mtrouter-2026}, or episode-level model selection under sparse rewards \citep{budget-aware-routing-2026}; concurrent analyses further presuppose diverse routing signals~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026}. These approaches, which operate in non-catastrophic settings, share the assumption that routing signal stems from diverse sources and a learned router can exploit it---an assumption that remains untested: no prior work has decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments.
7	
8	We propose a \emph{diagnostic framework} comprising (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding. Under ScienceWorld's penalty ($P{=}100$), DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.1\%, 99.0\%]$). The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic failure (``death,'' $-100$ penalty); once death episodes are removed, the gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$ (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
9	
10	This addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% tie rate; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \citep{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, comparing episode outcomes.
11	
12	Cross-context checks probe specificity: MATH Level~4--5 ($N{=}795$, code interpreter) shows no routing signal without catastrophic failure ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$); Llama-3.1-8B replication ($N{=}220$) confirms death dominance across model families (direction consistency 10/10; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}). Four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction) reduce death rates but none closes the gap to deterministic safety, with ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}).
13	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics, and predicts routing value from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (validated 4/4 conditions; Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry, a single mechanism that persists across four intervention axes and three model scales, revealing that in catastrophic-failure environments, signal diversity can collapse to a single dominant factor---death avoidance; an exploratory step-level router confirms this, recovering only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}); (3)~\textbf{cross-model convergence} across three 7--9B model families (DAF within $88$--$90\%$; Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-2-9B-it), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
```

```text
1. **[warning] Conclusion redundancy**: "stable across three model families (replicated across three 7–9B model families, spread 2.2 pp)" — "three model families" appears twice. Should be fixed.

2. **[warning] R26 W5 agent-floor regression**: The agent-floor discussion that was added for R26 W5 ("The 7 excluded agent-floor tasks—where both strategies achieve near-zero scores—represent a symmetric failure regime...") has been removed from Discussion §5. 

The §3 intro mentions "agent-floor performance (7)" as an exclusion reason, but the interpretive discussion of what this means for death dominance is gone—though this was an R26 fix and space is tight, so it might be adequately covered by §3's mention plus the Limitations section on environmental scope.

3. **[info] R27 Q4 (N=220 vs N=245)**: Not addressed. Should add footnote.

4. **[info] Figure 2 readability**: 0.38\text width might be small for a scatter plot—need to check visually.

5. **[info] Related work**: W6 additions look solid with MoE routing and counterfactual data augmentation now inline in ¶2, though Neural Module Networks and Process supervision weren't added.

6. **[info] Introduction M3**: L8 correctly includes "catastrophic failure ('death,' −100 penalty)".

7. **[Quality assessment of Discussion restructure]**: The three-paragraph structure works well—¶1 covers signal granularity comprehensively, ¶2 addresses non-triviality and oracle confound, and the organization flows logically through the key limitations.

8. **Abstract quality**: The rewrite is solid with 78.9% as the headline and a clear methodological vs empirical distinction, though it reads dense.

9. **§2.1 change**: The inline deterministic definition is clean, and "privileged-information strategy" functions as a descriptor rather than a replacement term.

10. **§3.1 changes**: Per-task correlation compressed well, and the sensitivity paragraph now includes cluster confidence intervals.

11. **§3.2 changes**: Internal vulnerability compressed to ~42% across both models.

12. **New bibliography entries**: Need to verify switch-transformer-2022, gshard-2021, and kaushik2020learning exist and are cited correctly.

The R26-N3 fix is still present in both the Introduction and Abstract—the language about signal diversity collapsing in catastrophic-failure environments is there. Overall assessment: strong execution with two issues to address—the Conclusion redundancy is a clear bug, and the agent-floor removal should be acknowledged—but the rest holds up well.
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	In ScienceWorld, death avoidance alone accounts for 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels, stable across three model families (replicated across three 7--9B model families, spread 2.2\,pp); our episode-level counterfactual framework diagnoses this before any router is built. Cross-environment probes provide preliminary evidence that the diagnostic procedure transfers; a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$). Future work should validate death dominance in additional catastrophic-failure environments and develop routing methods that exploit task-type-level signals.
4	
```

### Tool result

_source line 569 · 2026-05-22T05:22:32.668Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Cd5Dn7JvYrBeU4JqjNfFum`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. Six router variants (0.6B--4B, classification and generative) converge on near-chance step-level accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); this convergent failure suggests signal limitation, though extreme class imbalance (90.5\% deterministic-preferred, only 9 internal-preferred episodes) means a data-limited explanation remains viable---distinguishing these hypotheses requires ${\sim}$1{,}350 paired episodes to obtain 50 minority-class samples. Cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Training on pure-strategy labels but deploying under mixed-strategy routing also introduces a distribution shift~\citep{dagger-2011}; the negative result may partly reflect this train--deploy mismatch.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality and oracle confound.}
8	While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), two consequences were not predictable a priori: $10{\times}$ scaling to 72B fails to eliminate it (DAF${}= 92.4\%$), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\%$. All four interventions leave ${\geq}12$~pp residual gaps (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}); cross-model DR stability ($36$--$43\%$) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property. An oracle confound arises: is the signal merely ``use the oracle''? Two observations argue otherwise: (a)~$\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\% of the total gap)---on non-death episodes, internal reasoning provides almost no advantage; (b)~action-space filtering (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}) reduces death rate by 19~pp but remains far above deterministic safety (16.3\%).
9	
10	\paragraph{Practical implications and actionability.}
11	The penalty-invariant label fraction (78.9\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF varies with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Since routing is relevant in only 38.8\% of episodes (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}), even optimal routing yields modest gains (oracle margin 2.89 points; \S\ref{sec:baselines}). When $f_{\text{death}} > 75\%$, deterministic routing matches or exceeds learned alternatives at zero training cost; action-space filtering (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}) reduces death rate by 19~pp as a complementary intervention. The diagnostic identifies \emph{when} always-deterministic suffices versus when router development is warranted (as in ALFWorld, where DAF${=}0\%$).
12	
13	
```

</details>

```text
655-\paragraph{Prompt warning.} Adding an explicit system prompt (``use \texttt{focus on} only for final answers'') reduces aggregate death rate to 37.0\% ($\Delta = -11$~pp). Both identification tasks exhibit ironic effects (\texttt{identify-life-stages-1}: 35\%$\to$50\%; \texttt{identify-life-stages-2}: 25\%$\to$70\%), where explicit mention of the dangerous action may have increased its salience.
656-
657:\paragraph{Action-space filtering.} Blocking \texttt{focus on} during the exploration phase yields 379 blocks across 200 episodes with zero leaks and zero passthroughs (the agent never persisted in retrying blocked actions), reducing death rate to 29.0\% ($\Delta = -19$~pp). The zero-passthrough rate suggests \texttt{focus on} use is a shallow behavioral pattern rather than a deliberate strategy.
658-
659-\paragraph{Per-task variation.} Under filtering, \texttt{identify-life-stages} and \texttt{inclined-plane} approach deterministic-level safety (${\sim}$15--20\% DR), while \texttt{chemistry-mix} and \texttt{find-animal} remain at 35--40\%. Residual deaths occur during the answer-submission window, where the filter is inactive and agents submit \texttt{focus on} to incorrect objects.
--
```

```text
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex:7:Concurrent work examines \emph{when} to invoke tools: tool-call decisions correlate with uncertainty~\citep{tool-overuse-illusion-2026}, are encoded in hidden states~\citep{when2tool-2026}, impose a ``tool-use tax''~\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, or follow necessity-utility-affordability logic~\citep{to-call-or-not-2026}. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex:16:Of the 95 non-tie routing labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
```

```text
I'm noticing a potential mismatch here—the 29.0% result is specifically for the 10 death-prone tasks, while the 16.3% deterministic baseline is computed across all 11 tasks, so these aren't directly comparable metrics. The deterministic baseline for just those 10 death-prone tasks would be around 20.0%, not the 16.3% cited in the Discussion—but that's a pre-existing inconsistency rather than something introduced by the recent fixes, and the core claim about internal strategy performance being substantially worse still holds up. but not critical.

Wait, looking more carefully at the Discussion: it says action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19 percentage points but the internal strategy still sits at 29.0% on death-prone tasks versus the deterministic baseline at 16.3% overall. The comparison isn't perfectly apples-to-apples due to the task count difference, but the gap is substantial enough that it doesn't matter.

Let me verify these calculations: the 9.6% figure checks out, and I'm working through the base rate math—if internal-preferred occurs at 3.67% in the current data, getting to 50 instances would require around 1,362 samples, which aligns with the ~1,350 mentioned in the Discussion. The cluster-robust confidence interval of 73.3%-100.0% is confirmed in the Limitations section, and the 90.5% figure comes from 86 deterministic-preferred cases out of 95 non-ties.

I'm noticing the conclusion still has some redundancy—the point about stability across model families is being restated.
```

_source line 603 · 2026-05-22T07:47:36.902Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018DLCdCN545dmxdK9c8ZQNY`</summary>

```text
105	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic---an \emph{oracle degeneracy} where an always-deterministic lookup achieves 90.5\% of non-tie oracle decisions (balanced accuracy 50.0\%: perfect deterministic recall, zero internal recall).
106	
107	\subsection{Router Training: A Preliminary Negative Result}
108	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
109	
110	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. The near-chance step-level accuracy admits two interpretations: (A)~episode-level labels projected onto steps create ${\sim}$88\% uninformative signal (60.3\% pre-event neutral, 27.4\% post-event), drowning learnable patterns in noise; (B)~routing signal is genuinely task-type-level: there is no step-level pattern to learn. Directional conflict is only 0.3\% of 2{,}040 training steps, but the granularity mismatch leaves minimal signal. The text augmentation ablation (25\%--800\% via paraphrase, no improvement) and extreme class imbalance (86/95 $= 90.5\%$ deterministic-preferred) together favor Interpretation~B (non-learnability): no step-level learnable signal exists under current architectures and data regime.
111	
112	An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.001$, Bonferroni-corrected across 8 comparisons); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at chance. The 4B model's marginally above-chance episode-level accuracy is consistent with task-type identification rather than step-level routing signal, as confirmed by leave-one-task-type-out analysis degrading to chance (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below the majority-vote baseline of 50.0\%; this independent set includes held-out task types, so the below-chance result reflects cross-task transfer failure rather than overall router incompetence. Training uses episode-level splits (Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode leakage. The high uninformative rate connects to noisy-label learning~\citep{natarajan2013learning,northcutt2021confident,li2020dividemix}: standard ERM on labels with ${\sim}$88\% noise yields near-chance classifiers, consistent with theoretical bounds on learning from corrupted labels and empirical results in structured noise settings.
113	
114	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\ref{app:ablations}). A task-type-only classifier (logistic regression on one-hot task identity) achieves $64.7\%$ balanced accuracy---higher than all step-level routers---but collapses to chance under LOTO ($50.0\%$), matching the neural router's LOTO performance ($49.2\%$) and confirming that routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization.
115	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Signal granularity and router failure.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. Six router variants (0.6B--4B, classification and generative) converge on near-chance step-level accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); this convergent failure suggests signal limitation, though extreme class imbalance (90.5\% deterministic-preferred, only 9 internal-preferred episodes) means a data-limited explanation remains viable---distinguishing these hypotheses requires ${\sim}$1{,}350 paired episodes to obtain 50 minority-class samples. Cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\%; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Training on pure-strategy labels but deploying under mixed-strategy routing also introduces a distribution shift~\citep{dagger-2011}; the negative result may partly reflect this train--deploy mismatch.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality and oracle confound.}
8	While death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\S\ref{sec:daf}), two consequences were not predictable a priori: $10{\times}$ scaling to 72B fails to eliminate it (DAF${}= 92.4\%$), and Reflexion yields DAF${}>100\%$. All four interventions leave ${\geq}12$~pp residual gaps (Appendix~\ref{sec:threshold-routing}); cross-model DR stability ($36$--$43\%$) confirms death vulnerability is a task-environment property. An oracle confound arises: is the signal merely ``use the oracle''? Two observations argue otherwise: (a)~$\Delta_{\text{nd}} = 2.84$ (9.6\% of the total gap)---on non-death episodes, internal reasoning provides almost no advantage; (b)~action-space filtering (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}) reduces death rate by 19~pp but remains far above deterministic safety (16.3\%). The 7 excluded agent-floor tasks, where both strategies achieve near-zero scores, represent a symmetric failure regime that the framework correctly diagnoses as ``no signal.''
9	
10	\paragraph{Practical implications and actionability.}
11	The penalty-invariant label fraction (78.9\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF varies with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). When $f_{\text{death}} > 75\%$, always-deterministic matches learned alternatives at zero training cost (\S\ref{sec:baselines}); action-space filtering reduces death rate by 19~pp (Appendix~\ref{app:grounding-ablation}). The framework identifies \emph{when} always-deterministic suffices versus when router development is warranted (as in ALFWorld, where DAF${=}0\%$).
12	
13	
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	
3	\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}
4	A growing body of work augments language models with external tools \citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.
5	Self-supervised methods learn tool invocation from loss signals \citep{toolformer-2023,toolace-2025}, token-level methods embed tools in the vocabulary \citep{toolkengpt-2023}, task-level decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules \citep{art-2023,chameleon-2023}, and RL-based methods learn routing from reward \citep{retool-2025}.
6	All focus on \emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}
7	Concurrent work examines \emph{when} to invoke tools: tool-call decisions correlate with uncertainty~\citep{tool-overuse-illusion-2026}, are encoded in hidden states~\citep{when2tool-2026}, impose a ``tool-use tax''~\citep{tool-use-tax-2026}, or follow necessity-utility-affordability logic~\citep{to-call-or-not-2026}. Our decomposition yields a concrete answer: 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.
8	
9	\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}
10	Adaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \citep{graves2016act}. Mixture-of-Recursions \citep{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths in the LLM era; concurrent work further diversifies routing granularity: CogRouter \citep{cogrouter-2026} adapts per-step depth, ARES \citep{ares-2026} selects effort levels, and MTRouter \citep{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn settings.
11	RouteLLM \citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.
12	Concurrently, \citet{budget-aware-routing-2026} train episode-level routing policies under sparse terminal rewards, the closest setting to ours; they optimize \emph{which} model to invoke, whereas we diagnose \emph{what} drives the routing decision.
13	Mixture-of-Experts architectures~\citep{switch-transformer-2022,gshard-2021} learn token-level routing across expert sub-networks; we route between computation \emph{modes} rather than expert modules. Counterfactual data augmentation~\citep{kaushik2020learning} edits inputs to flip labels; our framework executes full counterfactual \emph{episodes} to decompose routing signal at the environment level.
14	
15	\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}
16	Prior work generates counterfactual explanations by perturbing individual actions \citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}; recent and concurrent extensions target LLM agents~\citep{pona2025abstract,axis-2026,cso-2026}. We instead execute complete episodes under two strategies to decompose routing signal \emph{before} training.
17	
18	\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}
19	ScienceWorld \citep{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; ALFWorld \citep{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary environment; AgentBench \citep{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. ReAct \citep{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \citep{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \citep{lats-2024} adds tree search. The concurrent SALT~\citep{salt-2026} assigns step-level credit on ALFWorld, complementing our episode-level decomposition.
20	
21	\paragraph{Safe RL and Causal Grounding.}
22	Death avoidance connects to constrained MDPs \citep{altman1999constrained}, risk-sensitive RL \citep{mihatsch2002risk}, and safe RL \citep{garcia2015comprehensive,achiam2017cpo,moldovan2012safe}; however, classical methods assume known dynamics or differentiable constraints, unavailable for LLM agents. \citet{ruan2024failures} identify catastrophic actions as a failure category; SafeRoute \citep{saferoute-2025} and Agent-SafetyBench \citep{agent-safetybench-2024} address model selection and systematic evaluation for agent safety. Our pure-strategy comparison is a simplified instance of the potential outcomes framework~\citep{rubin1974estimating}, eliminating confounding without structural causal models~\citep{pearl2009causality}. \citet{bonagiri2025check} show selective quitting yields safety gains; our framework explains \emph{why}---death avoidance dominates routing signal.
```

</details>

```text
"A task-type-only classifier (logistic regression on one-hot task identity) achieves 64.7% balanced accuracy—higher than all step-level routers—but collapses to chance under LOTO (50.0%), matching the neural router's LOTO performance (49.2%) and confirming that routing signal is a per-task-type constant with no cross-type generalization."


The framing correctly positions this as a signal granularity diagnosis rather than a positive control validation. However, I'm only seeing the 64.7% figure here—I need to track down where the 62.9% keyword variant appears, since Director specifically asked me to verify the distinction between these two numbers and their attributions.

Looking at the current text, only 64.7% shows up in §3.6, described as logistic regression on one-hot task identity, so there's no attribution confusion there. Scanning through Limitations, I see discussion of the ReAct baseline and Reflexion's performance (42.9% down to 31.4%), plus episode-level labels, but there's no explicit mention of alternative router architectures. However, §5 ¶1 does qualify the negative result by invoking data-limited explanations and distribution shift, which partially addresses the concern that other setups might behave differently. The paper seems to have shifted from explicitly listing alternative approaches to implicitly acknowledging them through the discussion of signal limitation and class imbalance.

For the oracle margin point in §5 ¶3, the key takeaway is that even with perfect routing decisions, the performance ceiling is only 2.89 percentage points higher than the current approach—suggesting the routing problem itself isn't the bottleneck.

Now I'm checking whether the removed details about routing relevance and internal model agreement are preserved elsewhere in the document. The 38.8% figure appears in §3.1, and the oracle margin is documented in §3.5, so the compression is justified even though the specific numbers don't appear in §5 anymore.

For the merged sentence on internal vulnerability, I'm verifying the percentages and differential magnitudes match across both models—Qwen and Llama show roughly 42% vulnerability with differential improvements of +26.5 and +30.0 percentage points respectively, which checks out.

Now I'm consolidating the Safe RL and Causal Grounding sections in Related Work, merging what were previously two separate discussions into a unified treatment that covers both safety frameworks and their causal foundations.

Verifying all 11 references are accounted for across the merged section—from the Safe RL citations through the causal grounding work—nothing's been dropped.

I'm checking whether the Conclusion still lands on page 8 after these insertions, since the previous compilation showed 8 pages total. 1	\section{Conclusion}
```
```text
2	
3	In ScienceWorld, death avoidance alone accounts for 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels, replicated across three 7--9B model families (spread 2.2\,pp); our episode-level counterfactual framework diagnoses this before any router is built. Cross-environment probes provide preliminary evidence that the diagnostic procedure transfers; a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$). Future work should validate death dominance in additional catastrophic-failure environments and develop routing methods that exploit task-type-level signals.
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Wd6XRHNfmUDxf1CQ2psYrv`</summary>

```text
411	\end{table}
412	
413	The contrast between step-level ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) and episode-level ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$) evaluation reveals a generalization gap: the step-level signal likely reflects memorization of episode-specific context (enabled by same-episode leakage) rather than extraction of generalizable routing features. At episode level, the router performs below chance across all 8 seeds (range: 43.1\%--49.6\%). Paraphrase-based text augmentation (Table~\ref{tab:text-augmentation}) from 25\% to 800\% ($32{\times}$ range) provides no improvement, converging to the always-deterministic baseline ($50\%$). Paraphrase augmentation inflates the majority class without generating novel routing patterns; genuine episode-level scaling (collecting more counterfactual pairs) remains an open question. With DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), always-deterministic trivially solves the episode-level routing problem, leaving the step-level router with no exploitable signal under current architectures.
414	
415	\paragraph{Text augmentation ablation.} To test whether the episode-level generalization failure stems from data scarcity, we vary training set size from 25\% to 800\% of the original via paraphrase-based text augmentation (synonym substitution + template variation on step observations), holding the episode-level validation set fixed. This tests augmentation-based scaling, not genuine episode-level scaling (which would require new counterfactual collections). Table~\ref{tab:text-augmentation} reports the results.
```

```text
435	\end{table}
436	
437	All scales hover at ${\sim}50\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy (equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline), with no monotonic improvement as data increases. Augmentation-based scaling provides no improvement at any scale; however, episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics, a $5.5{\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\%$ signal. The failure points to a limitation of the current data distribution: when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, and the step-level router finds no exploitable signal under the current architecture and data regime.
438	
439	Augmentation disproportionately increases the majority (deterministic) class: Det\% rises from 89.6\% at 100\% to 94.2\% at 200\% and ${\sim}97.7\%$ at 800\%. However, the complete collapse to majority-class prediction (internal recall $= 0.0$) occurs even at the smallest unaugmented scale (25\%, ${\sim}90\%$ det); this is not an augmentation artifact. Balanced accuracy corrects for class imbalance by averaging per-class recall, so the metric itself is not biased by the skew.
```

_source line 636 · 2026-05-22T09:05:39.478Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0186rgsgVardzcZnWqUcVd9A`</summary>

```text
I've successfully read the complete Round 28 review report. Here's the comprehensive content:

## Report Summary

**Paper Title**: "Death Avoidance as the Dominant Routing Signal: A Counterfactual Analysis on ScienceWorld"

**Venue**: EMNLP 2026 (ARR May cycle), Interpretability and Analysis track

**Overall Score**: 3.0 (Findings)

**Review Mode**: Fresh, with 2 reviewers (R1: methodology rigor + experimental design; R2: novelty + completeness + writing)

---

## Key Findings

**Core Discovery**: 
- Episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework decomposes routing signals by comparing pure-strategy outcomes
- 78.9% of non-tie routing labels originate from death asymmetry (internal strategy triggers -100 death penalty while deterministic strategy survives)
- Cross-model replication (Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-2-9B-it) confirms 88-90% Death Avoidance Fraction (DAF) with 2.2pp spread
- Router training failure: episode-level balanced accuracy 47.1% (below 50% chance)

---

## Critical Issues (Must-Fix) - 2 items

1. **DAF P-dependence insufficiently discussed in main text**: DAF mathematically increases with penalty P (P=10→50.4%, P=50→82.6%, P=100→90.4%, P=200→94.9%). While label fraction (78.9%) is P-invariant, the paper treats DAF as core finding but relegates P-sensitivity discussion to appendix. Recommendation: Add 1-2 sentences in §2.4 discussing P-sensitivity numerically, lower DAF prominence in Abstract/Conclusion.

2. **"Deterministic" semantics vary across environments**: Three environments implement "deterministic" differently (oracle simulator / code interpreter / admissible-action enumeration) with incomparable information advantages. Conclusion claims "diagnostic procedure transfers" without precise qualification. Recommendation: Add clarification that transfer applies to the procedure, not the deterministic computation concept.

---

## Should-Fix Issues - 7 items

1. **W1**: 63% task type exclusion rate needs fuller justification in main text (currently one sentence)
2. **W2**: Episode→step granularity mismatch (R1 critical, R2 warning; downgraded to warning). Abstract should add "episode-level" qualifier
3. **W3**: Router negative result's signal-limited vs data-limited distinction incomplete; need more symmetric presentation
4. **W4**: K=11 cluster bootstrap limitations not sufficiently flagged (should reference Cameron et al. K≥20-50 guidance)
5. **W5**: 72B scaling only probed, not comprehensively evaluated; needs scope limitation in Limitations section
6. **W6**: "Death" terminology limits generality; graded failure scenarios not discussed
7. **W7**: Missing qualitative discussion of concurrent routing methods (CogRouter/ARES/MTRouter) in death-dominated environments

---

## Minor Issues - 5 items

- **M1**: Figure 2 has 11 points with no task name labels
- **M2**: §1 "signal diversity can collapse" phrase repeats
- **M3**: Pages 7-8 contain two consecutive pages of pure text with no display items
- **M4**: Missing references (4 total):
  - Alshiekh et al. (2018) Safe RL via Shielding
  - Geifman & El-Yaniv (2017) Selective Classification
  - Schuster et al. (2022) CALM
  - Raposo et al. (2024) Mixture-of-Depths
- **M5**: Related Work lacks non-agent adaptive computation (early exit, speculative decoding)

---

## Three Explicit Questions to Authors

1. **P=0 label fraction stability**: How does the 78.9% label fraction hold at P=0? The paper mentions 54 "sole-signal labels" degrade to ties at P=0. Algorithm 1's death-first priority is equivalent to infinite penalty, creating tension with "penalty-independent" narrative.

2. **150 no-preference episodes' action sequences**: Did the two strategies follow similar paths despite same outcomes? Different paths could indicate step-level routing signals masked by episode-level aggregation.

3. **72B non-death signal enhancement**: At 72B, death rate drops to 30.6%. Does Δ_nd increase significantly? If so, does this qualify conclusions about death dominance's scale-robustness?

---

## Reviewer Consensus (Complete Agreement - 8 items)

1. DAF P-dependence is the critical issue requiring author response
2. "Deterministic" needs precise cross-environment qualification
3. 63% exclusion rate needs fuller main text justification
4. K=11 cluster bootstrap limitations need explicit flagging
5. 72B has limited scope
6. Robustness verification (20 appendices) is top-tier for analysis track
7. Negative router result reporting is honest and valuable
8. Cross-model convergence (3 families, 88-90% DAF) strongly excludes model-specificity

---

## Reviewer Divergence (Resolved)

**Episode→step granularity**: R1 marked critical; R2 marked minor framing. **Final**: Downgraded to warning. R2's evidence: 97.4% step ties make episode-level principled; 61.2% no-preference also diagnostic; §3.6 and §5 discuss step-level failure. Solution: Add "episode-level" qualifier to Abstract.

**Limitations length**: R2 wanted compression; R1 REBUT noting honest limitations are an asset in analysis papers. **Final**: No weakness—R1 reasoning accepted.

**Router architecture simplicity**: R1 warning on limited scope; R2 REBUT with 6 variants + LOTO + death-proximal labeling, but AGREE on more precise scope framing. **Final**: Kept as W3 sub-issue with recommendation for more symmetric dual-explanation presentation.

---

## Scoring Rationale

**3.0 (Findings)** – not main conference due to:

1. **Excitement deficit**: Core finding (death avoidance dominates in catastrophic-failure environments) has limited surprise value—it's somewhat a priori expected
2. **DAF mathematical artifact**: Core metric partially determined by P=100 choice (50.4% at P=10)
3. **Single-environment primary validation**: Full verification only on ScienceWorld; MATH/ALFWorld are boundary probes with incomparable "deterministic" implementations
4. **Actionability gap**: Diagnostic framework doesn't prescribe practical next steps beyond "use always-deterministic"

Both reviewers independently scored 3.5 (Borderline Conference), but excitement deficit is structural since Round 16.

---

## Visual Layout Feedback

**3 Warnings**:
1. Pages 7-8 contain pure text with no display items
2. Page 9 Limitations quite large (5 subsections)
3. Page 24 last page underutilized

**Positive findings**: No LaTeX errors, proper formatting, reasonable display items for diagnostic paper, consistent color semantics, booktabs style.

---

## Detailed Section Comments

- **§1**: "Signal diversity collapse" phrase repeats; contributions list items too long
- **§2.4**: DAF definition needs immediate P-sensitivity discussion with numerical examples
- **§3**: First paragraph should expand on 63% exclusion (include excluded ΔDR stats and taxonomy definition); Figure 2 needs task name labels; §3.4 ALFWorld treatment too sparse; §3.6 router architecture scope should be explicit
- **§4**: Missing early-exit and per-token adaptive computation references
- **§5**: Signal-limited vs data-limited explanations need more symmetric treatment; add concurrent routing methods discussion; discuss graded failure scenarios
- **§6**: "Diagnostic procedure transfers" needs qualification clarification
- **Limitations**: Should explicitly reference Cameron et al. K≥20-50 guidance; add 72B scope limitations
```

</details>

```text
14	
15	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate), decomposes routing signal via penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics, and predicts routing value from a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot (validated 4/4 conditions; Appendix~\ref{app:decision-framework}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry, a single mechanism that persists across four intervention axes and three model scales, revealing that in catastrophic-failure environments, signal diversity can collapse to a single dominant factor---death avoidance; an exploratory step-level router confirms this, recovering only task-type classification (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}); (3)~\textbf{cross-model convergence} across three 7--9B model families (DAF within $88$--$90\%$; Llama-3.1-8B, Gemma-2-9B-it), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
16	
17	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UV1nsDuipX6PXCAUe6V8cz`</summary>

```text
58	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
59	\begin{equation}
60	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
61	\end{equation}%
62	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
63	
64	Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}).
65	
66	\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}
67	DAF (90.4\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \gg$ typical non-death $\Delta{\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.
68	
69	\paragraph{Statistical inference.}
70	We quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}.
71	
72	We also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}), reported in \S\ref{sec:death-decomp}.
73	
74	\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}
75	At step level, 97.4\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\S\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
```

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types (63\%) lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). This scope limits generalizability: conclusions apply to the included subset, not to all ScienceWorld tasks. An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}). Extended validation at $n{=}20$ on five representative excluded tasks confirms this direction ($\overline{\Delta\text{DR}} = +32.0$\,pp; Appendix~\ref{app:extended-validation}). A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference between internal strategy variants ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}). Prescreening used $n{=}5$ episodes per task type; directional consistency (18/19 types favoring deterministic) rather than point estimates drives the inclusion criterion.
5	
6	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
```

```text
Now I have all the context needed. Let me compile my assessment for the 6 questions.

### Q1: W-Critical-1 — Is §2.4 addition sufficient?

Current §2.4 (method.tex L64): "Since DAF = δP/(δP + Δ_nd), it increases monotonically with P, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding."

Current §3.1 (experiments.tex L18): "DAF varies with penalty magnitude (50.4% at P=10; 82.6% at P=50; Appendix K)"


The director's proposal to add the P-sensitivity numbers directly to §2.4 would work well. This placement puts the concrete evidence right where DAF is defined, and combined with the abstract's headline figure, the acknowledgment of monotonicity, and the discussion's focus on penalty-invariant diagnostics, it creates a coherent narrative that the metric depends on penalty choice rather than being a fundamental property.

However, I'd recommend removing those same numbers from §3.1 afterward to eliminate duplication and tighten the space—§3.1 can just point readers back to §2.4 and the appendix instead.

On the second question about §3's exclusion rationale: the section already covers substantial ground with five sentences on the exclusion logic, anti-cherry-picking safeguards, extended validation, ablation studies, and prescreening. The director wants to add two more sentences defining taxonomic redundancy and discussing excluded ΔDR, but that ΔDR figure is already mentioned in the current text. The only genuinely new content would be the redundancy definition, which doesn't seem essential enough to justify expanding what's already a dense paragraph.

For W7, I think the two-line addition is worthwhile—it takes the diagnostic insight and projects it onto real methods like CogRouter, ARES, and MTRouter, showing what the findings actually mean for existing approaches. That's the kind of forward-looking analysis that strengthens the discussion, and the space cost is minimal.

On the page budget, I'm looking at a net deficit of about 1-2 lines after accounting for the new content and compressions. The optimizations I'm considering—moving W-C1 numbers into section 2.4 to free up space in 3.1, and tightening W1 to a single sentence—should get me close to breaking even. I need to be strategic about where the cuts happen to preserve the most impactful additions.

For the R28 Q1 point about the penalty-independent labeling, there's a real tension here: the labeling function doesn't explicitly use the penalty parameter, but it bakes in an assumption that death is always the worst outcome, which is functionally equivalent to setting P to infinity. This creates a mismatch when P=0—the labels would treat death as catastrophic while the scoring system treats it as neutral, which would flip 54 episodes from decisive to tied outcomes. I can compress this to clarify that the labeling is penalty-independent in form but penalty-dependent in assumption. The paper does acknowledge this limitation in section 3.1 where it mentions the ties that emerge at P=0. For the rebuttal, I should address Q2 about the 150 no-preference action sequences—while I don't have step-level breakdowns of those episodes, the 97.4% step-tie rate indicates that individual steps diverge without affecting final outcomes, which aligns with treating them as noise. I'm ready to post the response now.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Pt9oneneZRcdoF9hik5RKw`</summary>

```text
58	We define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:
59	\begin{equation}
60	    \text{DAF} = 1 - \frac{\Delta_{\text{nd}}}{\Delta_{\text{full}}} \approx \frac{\delta P}{\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}}
61	\end{equation}%
62	\footnote{When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}
63	
64	Since DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. For context: DAF $= 50.4\%$ at $P{=}10$, $82.6\%$ at $P{=}50$, and $90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ (full curve in Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). We treat the label fraction (78.9\%) as the primary, penalty-invariant diagnostic and DAF as a supplementary, environment-specific measure. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design~\citep{ng1999policy} (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}).
65	
66	\paragraph{Relationship between DAF and label fraction.}
67	DAF (90.4\%) exceeds the label fraction (78.9\%) because death episodes carry disproportionately large score gaps ($P{=}100 \gg$ typical non-death $\Delta{\approx}29$), amplifying their weight in score-weighted aggregation. The label fraction, invariant to penalty magnitude, serves as the primary finding; DAF reflects the score-space reality under ScienceWorld's reward structure.
68	
69	\paragraph{Statistical inference.}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01DeuX1ywvAXyyintkhkasWK`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM agent routing (deciding when to rely on internal reasoning versus external tool use) is typically addressed by training routers directly, without first understanding what mechanistic signal drives the routing preference. This absence of diagnostic methods risks building routers atop confounded or trivial signal. We propose an episode-level counterfactual framework that compares pure-strategy outcomes (internal-only vs.\ deterministic-only) to decompose routing signal into its mechanistic components before any router is constructed. Applying this framework to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes), we find that 78.9\% of non-tie episode-level routing labels arise from death asymmetry---a finding replicated across three architecturally distinct 7--9B model families (spread 2.2\,pp), revealing that in catastrophic-failure environments, signal diversity can collapse to a single dominant mechanism. Cross-environment probes on MATH and ALFWorld diagnose qualitatively different routing mechanisms (no extractable signal and information asymmetry, respectively), providing preliminary evidence of applicability beyond death-dominated settings. The framework distinguishes \emph{methodological generality} (applicable to any environment with paired episode outcomes) from \emph{empirical generality} (full quantitative validation on ScienceWorld only). A ${\sim}$50-episode diagnostic pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$); environments with smaller effects may require larger samples.
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

_source line 661 · 2026-05-22T09:20:33.480Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Uuj85RnbvE9wYcWXyr2A3T`</summary>

```text
15	
16	Of the 95 non-tie routing labels, 75 (78.9\%) arise from death asymmetry: the internal strategy triggers a catastrophic $-100$ penalty while the deterministic strategy survives. This penalty-independent label fraction is the primary diagnostic metric. Of these, 54 are sole-signal labels where death was the only discriminator; at $P{=}0$ these reduce to ties, but the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}).
17	
18	Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\%) show no preference between strategies; routing is relevant in the remaining 95 (38.8\%). Among these, death avoidance dominates. The Death Avoidance Factor, a derived penalty-dependent measure, quantifies death's contribution to the total performance gap: DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$ ($\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$; 95\% CI: $[81.1\%, 99.0\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies monotonically with $P$ (\S\ref{sec:daf}; Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\Delta_{\text{nd}} \to 0$, DAF $\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\Delta_{\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\geq}3$; \S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.
19	
20	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
21	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$). Per-task death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}); since DAF$\to 1$ implies $r \to 1$ by construction, the near-perfect correlation is a consistency check, not an independent finding (Appendix~\ref{app:correlation}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
22	
23	\paragraph{Signal concentration and binary structure.}
24	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. The aggregate 78.9\% reflects a per-task binary structure: all 10 death-prone task types produce 100\% death-asymmetry labels, while \texttt{find-non-living-thing} (zero deaths, $\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores) produces 0\%. The framework thus diagnoses a binary property (``is this environment death-dominated?'') rather than a continuous routing signal. Removing \texttt{find-non-living-thing} raises the label fraction to 100\% and yields DAF $\geq$100\% ($\Delta_\text{nd} \leq 0$; death avoidance fully accounts for the routing gap among remaining task types; Appendix~\ref{app:loto-daf}).
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	In ScienceWorld, death avoidance alone accounts for 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels, replicated across three 7--9B model families (spread 2.2\,pp); our episode-level counterfactual framework diagnoses this before any router is built. Cross-environment probes provide preliminary evidence that the diagnostic procedure---though not the specific operationalization of ``deterministic''---transfers across environments with qualitatively different information asymmetries; a ${\sim}$50-episode pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$). Future work should validate death dominance in additional catastrophic-failure environments and develop routing methods that exploit task-type-level signals.
4	
```

   - **Label fraction 78.9%**: 75/95 non-tie labels from death asymmetry, penalty-independent primary metric
   - **Episode-level counterfactual framework**: Pure-strategy comparison (internal vs deterministic)
   - **Cross-model validation**: Qwen2.5-7B (DAF=90.4%), Llama-3.1-8B (DAF=88.2%), Gemma-2-9B (DAF=89.7%)
   - **72B scaling**: DAF=92.4%, Δ_nd=+2.28, ΔDR=+27.8pp
   - **Router negative result**: Step-level BA 56-59%, LOTO 49.2%, positioned as "Preliminary"
   - **W4 task-type positive control**: LogReg on one-hot = 64.7% BA, keyword variant = 62.9%
   - **97.4% step tie rate**: Per-step oracle degeneracy
   - **D131/D132**: Experiment freeze, no new GPU experiments before DDL
   - **P-sensitivity**: DAF=50.4% at P=10, 82.6% at P=50, 90.4% at P=100 (now in §2.4)
   - **Signal/data-limited dual interpretation**: 90.5% class imbalance, ~1,350 episodes needed for 50 minority samples
   - **Oracle confound**: Δ_nd=2.84 (9.6% of total gap) + App O filtering 19pp
   - **P=0 analysis**: 54 sole-signal→ties, 41 remaining non-tie, 21/41=51.2% death-asymmetric

```json
        "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM agent routing (deciding when to rely on internal reasoning versus external tool use) is typically addressed by training routers directly, without first understanding what mechanistic signal drives the routing preference. This absence of diagnostic methods risks building routers atop confounded or trivial signal. We propose an episode-level counterfactual framework that compares pure-strategy outcomes (internal-only vs.\\ deterministic-only) to decompose routing signal into its mechanistic components before any router is constructed. Applying this framework to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes), we find that 78.9\\% of non-tie episode-level routing labels arise from death asymmetry---a finding replicated across three architecturally distinct 7--9B model families (spread 2.2\\,pp), revealing that in catastrophic-failure environments, signal diversity can collapse to a single dominant mechanism. Cross-environment probes on MATH and ALFWorld diagnose qualitatively different routing mechanisms (no extractable signal and information asymmetry, respectively), providing preliminary evidence of applicability beyond death-dominated settings. The framework distinguishes \\emph{methodological generality} (applicable to any environment with paired episode outcomes) from \\emph{empirical generality} (full quantitative validation on ScienceWorld only). A ${\\sim}$50-episode diagnostic pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$); environments with smaller effects may require larger samples.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/abstract.tex"
  },
```

```json
        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nIn ScienceWorld, death avoidance alone accounts for 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels, replicated across three 7--9B model families (spread 2.2\\,pp); our episode-level counterfactual framework diagnoses this before any router is built. Cross-environment probes provide preliminary evidence that the diagnostic procedure---though not the specific operationalization of ``deterministic''---transfers across environments with qualitatively different information asymmetries; a ${\\sim}$50-episode pilot provides preliminary build-vs-buy signal given large effect sizes ($d = 3.02$). Future work should validate death dominance in additional catastrophic-failure environments and develop routing methods that exploit task-type-level signals.\n",
        "numLines": 4,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 4
      }
```
